PART II

ROYAL VISITS TO COMMONWEALTH COUNTRIES OVERSEAS 59

ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT IN COMMONWEALTH COUNTRIES OVERSEAS 63

IMPERIAL AND COMMONWEALTH CONFERENCES 68
ROYAL VISITS TO COMMONWEALTH COUNTRIES OVERSEAS

Visits by Members of the Royal Family to parts of the Commonwealth other than the United Kingdom are a comparatively recent development. During the 19th Century the only formal visits of this kind were those paid by King Edward VII, as Prince of Wales, to Canada (and the United States) in 1860 and to India in 1875. There were, however, occasions on which Members of the Royal Family serving in the Armed Forces of the Crown spent part of their service in overseas countries of the Empire and so had some opportunity of becoming acquainted with the Empire overseas. Thus Queen Victoria's second son the Duke of Edinburgh, while serving in the Royal Navy, visited Australia and New Zealand in 1869.

With the development of more rapid means of communication, such visits have become during the present century more easily arranged and consequently more frequent. The first was the Royal Tour undertaken by the Duke and Duchess of Cornwall and York (afterwards King George V and Queen Mary) in 1901. The primary purpose of the visit was the opening of the first Parliament of the Commonwealth of Australia, but it was decided that it should also be extended to New Zealand and Canada. The Tour lasted from the 15th March to the 2nd November, 1901. During its course, Their Royal Highnesses visited also certain non-self-governing Colonies (Gibraltar, Malta, Ceylon, the Straits Settlements and Mauritius) as well as Natal, Cape Colony and Newfoundland. In Australia, they visited each of the six States and, in addition to attending various official functions in the capital cities, paid visits to several country towns. Similarly in New Zealand they visited both the principal cities and some of the country towns. The Canadian tour included the majority of the Provinces from the Atlantic to the Pacific.

In 1905, King George V, as Prince of Wales, visited India with the Princess of Wales. The Tour lasted from the 19th October, 1905 until the 8th May, 1906. Their Royal Highnesses visited the principal cities in India (which at that time included Burma), with numerous halts at smaller places on the route and the Prince of Wales, in addition to attending ceremonial functions, met many of the leading personalities including not only those in official positions but also the President of the Indian National Congress, Mr. Gokhale.

In July 1908 His Royal Highness paid a short visit to Quebec (unaccompanied by the Princess of Wales) in order to attend the Tercentenary celebrations.

In 1910 it was contemplated that the Prince of Wales should visit South Africa and open the first Parliament of the Union but, owing to his Accession to the Throne on the death of King Edward VII, this visit had to be abandoned, and the ceremony was performed by the Duke of Connaught as His Majesty's special representative.

After his Accession to the Throne, King George V with Queen Mary visited India for the Delhi Durbar. The visit lasted from the 11th November, 1911 to the 5th February, 1912. In addition to the elaborate ceremonies connected with the Durbar itself, Their Majesties paid a visit to Calcutta.

This was the only visit by King George V as Sovereign to any part of His Majesty's overseas dominions. In 1912 there was a suggestion that he might visit South Africa, but United Kingdom Ministers advised against the Sovereign's absence from the country for a considerable period at a time of domestic political crisis and international tension, and the visit did not take place.

King Edward VIII, as Prince of Wales, paid a series of visits to the Dominions and India. On the 5th August, 1919 he left for Canada in H.M.S. Renown and arrived back on the 1st December, 1919. During the outward journey he visited Newfoundland. While in Canada, in addition to laying the cornerstone of the new Parliament Buildings at Ottawa and attending numerous official functions, he visited
over 50 towns as well as many smaller places and met members of all classes of the community. On the 16th March, 1920 he left, again in H.M.S. Renown, for a similar tour of Australia and New Zealand. During the journeys, outward and homeward, he visited also Barbados, Fiji, Samoa, Trinidad, British Guiana, Grenada, St. Lucia, Dominica, Antigua and Bermuda. He arrived back in England on the 11th October, 1920.

On the 26th October, 1921 His Royal Highness left in H.M.S. Renown on a visit to India, calling on the way at Gibraltar and Malta. In India he visited Bombay, Poona, Baroda, Rajputana, Jodhpur, Bikaner, Lucknow, Allahabad, Benares, Calcutta, Rangoon, Mandalay, Madras, Mysore, Hyderabad, Indore, Bhopal, Gwalior, Agra, Delhi, Lahore, Kashmir, Peshawar and Karachi. Thereafter he visited Ceylon, Malaya, Hong Kong, North Borneo and Brunei, and he returned to England on the 20th June, 1922.

On the 28th March, 1925 His Royal Highness left in H.M.S. Repulse for South Africa. In the course of the outward voyage he visited the Gambia, Sierra Leone, the Gold Coast and Nigeria. In South Africa he visited each of the four Provinces of the Union, the High Commission Territories and Southern Rhodesia, as well as Northern Rhodesia. On the return journey he paid a brief visit to St. Helena. He arrived back in England on the 16th October, 1925.

In 1928 the Prince of Wales, in company with the Duke of Gloucester, visited East Africa. He returned suddenly in December without completing the visit, owing to the serious illness of King George V, but continued the visit in the following year, spending brief periods in the Union of South Africa and Southern Rhodesia.

In addition to these official visits, His Royal Highness paid occasional private visits to his ranch in Canada.

In 1927 the Duke of York (afterwards King George VI) received a Commission from the King to open the first meeting of the Parliament of the Commonwealth of Australia to be held in the new capital city of Canberra. Accompanied by the Duchess of York (now Queen Elizabeth the Queen Mother) he left England in H.M.S. Renown on the 6th January, 1927. Before proceeding to Australia, Their Royal Highnesses paid a visit, lasting three weeks, to New Zealand, calling on the way at Jamaica and Fiji. Owing to ill-health the Duchess was unable to carry out most of the tour of the South Island, but otherwise Their Royal Highnesses visited all the chief towns, both large and small, in New Zealand. The Australian visit lasted for a little less than two months and, in addition to attending the elaborate ceremonies connected with the Inauguration of Canberra and numerous official functions in each of the State capitals, Their Royal Highnesses were able to visit some of the country towns. On the return journey they visited Mauritius, Malta and Gibraltar. They arrived back on the 27th June, 1927.

In 1934 Prince George (afterwards Duke of Kent) visited South Africa. He left on the 19th January, 1934 on the R.M.S. Carnarvon Castle and visited the principal towns in the Union of South Africa as well as the High Commission Territories and Southern and Northern Rhodesia. He arrived back in England on the 23rd April, 1934.

In addition to these visits, Members of the Royal Family have on several occasions been appointed Governors-General of Dominions. The Duke of Connaught, who as stated above had opened the first Parliament of the Union of South Africa in 1910, was Governor-General of Canada from 1911 to 1916. Prince Arthur of Connaught was Governor-General of the Union of South Africa from 1920 to 1924. The Earl of Athlone was Governor-General of the Union of South Africa from 1924 to 1930 and Governor-General of Canada from 1940 to 1946. In 1939 the King approved the appointment of the late Duke of Kent to be Governor-General of the Commonwealth of Australia, but owing to the outbreak of war His Royal Highness never took up the appointment. The Duke of Gloucester was appointed Governor-General in 1944 and held the post until 1947.
The first visit by a reigning Sovereign to any Dominion was that paid by King George VI, accompanied by Queen Elizabeth (now Queen Elizabeth the Queen Mother) to Canada in 1939. Their Majesties left England in the liner Empress of Australia on the 6th May, 1939 and arrived back in the liner Empress of Britain on the 21st June, 1939. They crossed the whole of Canada in the Royal Train, halting at various towns on the way in order that the greatest number of persons might have an opportunity of seeing them, in addition to spending longer periods in Ottawa and the various Provincial capitals where they attended numerous official functions. A significant feature of the visit was the fact that the King, as Sovereign of Canada, performed personally certain acts normally performed by the Governor-General as His Majesty's Representative, viz. the giving of the Royal Assent to a number of Bills passed by both Houses of the Canadian Parliament and the reception of the new United States Minister for the purpose of presenting his Letters of Credence. The date of official celebration of the King's Birthday (20th May) fell within the period of the visit and this was marked by special observances in Ottawa, including a ceremony of Trooping the Colour. Other events which may be specially mentioned were the unveiling by His Majesty of the Canadian National War Memorial and the laying by the Queen of the cornerstone of the new Supreme Court Building. While in Canada, Their Majesties also paid a few days' visit to the United States, and on the return journey they visited Newfoundland.

In 1947 King George VI, accompanied by the Queen, Princess Elizabeth (now Queen Elizabeth II) and Princess Margaret, visited South Africa. The Royal Party left England in H.M.S. Vanguard on the 1st February, 1947 and arrived back on the 10th May, 1947. As in Canada in 1939, the King performed personally in the Union of South Africa certain acts normally performed by the Governor-General, viz. the Opening of Parliament and signification of approval, at a meeting of the Executive Council, of a number of Government Orders. The Royal Party visited in a special train all the principal cities in the Union, attending numerous functions of every kind and seeing something of the industrial and agricultural life of the country as well as native ceremonies. They also visited Southern Rhodesia, where His Majesty opened Parliament, and the High Commission Territories, and paid a brief visit to Northern Rhodesia. A visit was paid to St. Helena on the way back. During the visit to South Africa Princess Elizabeth's 21st Birthday occurred (21st April) and Her Royal Highness, after taking the salute at a special military parade, made to the Commonwealth the notable broadcast in which she declared her resolve to devote her life to its service.

It was contemplated that the King, with the Queen and Princess Margaret, should visit Australia and New Zealand in the early part of 1949 but, owing to the state of His Majesty's health, the visit had to be cancelled. In 1951 arrangements were made for a visit to Australia, New Zealand and Ceylon but, as a result of His Majesty's operation in September 1951, it became impossible for him to carry out his intention and he asked Princess Elizabeth and the Duke of Edinburgh to pay these visits on his behalf. Their Royal Highnesses left England on the 31st January, 1952 but had only reached Kenya when, owing to the King's sudden death and Princess Elizabeth's Accession, it became necessary for her return immediately, and the Tour was consequently abandoned.

Previously, in October and November 1951, Princess Elizabeth and the Duke of Edinburgh had paid a visit to Canada. They left by air on the 8th October, 1951 and spent five weeks in Canada, returning from Newfoundland by the liner Empress of Scotland. In addition to attending official functions in Ottawa, including the presentation of Queen Mary's carpet to the National Gallery of Canada, they visited towns in all the Canadian Provinces, including Newfoundland, and paid a brief visit to the United States.

Early in 1952 King George VI accepted an invitation from the Prime Minister of the Union of South Africa (Dr. Malan) to pay a short private visit to South Africa for the purpose of convalescence and to stay at the official residence of the
Union Prime Minister in Natal but, owing to His Majesty’s death, this visit never took place.

Two visits by Members of the Royal Family on special occasions during recent years have been that of the Duke of Gloucester to Ceylon in 1948 for the opening of the first Parliament of the Island after its attainment of independent membership of the Commonwealth, and that of Queen Elizabeth the Queen Mother with Princess Margaret to Southern Rhodesia in 1953 for the opening of the Rhodes Centenary Exhibition, when Her Majesty also laid the foundation stone of the Rhodesia University College.

The recent visit of Queen Elizabeth II and the Duke of Edinburgh to Australia, New Zealand and Ceylon was the first visit by a reigning Sovereign to those countries and the first occasion on which Her Majesty personally had visited any of them. The Royal Party left London by air on the 23rd November, 1953 and arrived back on the 15th May, 1954. The journey was carried out partly by air, partly in the liner Gothic and partly in the Royal Yacht Britannia. Colonies and Territories under Her Majesty’s Protection visited in the course of the Tour were Bermuda, Jamaica, Fiji, Tonga, Cocos Islands, Aden, Uganda, Malta and Gibraltar. The Royal Party spent 5½ weeks in New Zealand, two months in Australia and ten days in Ceylon, visiting over 50 different cities and towns in New Zealand and some 60 in Australia including the capitals of all the Australian States as well as the Federal capital, Canberra. In Ceylon they visited Polonnarwa, Sigiriya, Nuwara Eliya and Kandy as well as Colombo.

Her Majesty’s position as Queen of Australia, of New Zealand and of Ceylon, no less than as Queen of the United Kingdom, as signified in the forms of the Royal Style and Titles adopted in the several countries, was illustrated by her performance personally of certain functions normally performed on her behalf by the Governor-General. In each of these countries Her Majesty formally opened Parliament and held Investitures. In New Zealand she gave the Royal Assent to a Bill passed by both Houses of Parliament and also presided at a meeting of the Executive Council where she signified approval of a number of Government Orders. In Australia Her Majesty presided over a meeting of the Federal Executive Council at Canberra. She also opened Parliament in several of the Australian States (New South Wales, Victoria, South Australia and Tasmania) and presided at meetings of the Executive Council in New South Wales, South Australia, Queensland and Western Australia.

Another special feature of the Tour was the holding of Privy Councils in each of the three countries for the purpose of swearing in certain local personalities as Privy Counsellors. These were the first instances of meetings of the United Kingdom Privy Council in any other Member country of the Commonwealth. In Australia and New Zealand the respective Prime Ministers, and in Ceylon the Governor-General, all of whom were already Privy Councillors, acted as Lord President of the Council for the occasion.

In addition to official functions of the normal kind, special events which may be mentioned were the delivery of the Queen’s Broadcast to the Commonwealth on Christmas Day from Auckland in New Zealand; the attendance of the Duke of Edinburgh at the funeral of 21 unidentified victims of the Tangiwai Rail Disaster in New Zealand; the laying of the foundation stone for the New Anglican Cathedral in Wellington; the unveiling by the Queen at Canberra of the Australian National Memorial to the United States of America and the dedication of the forecourt of the Shrine of Remembrance at Melbourne; the Royal Perahera and visit to the Temple of the Tooth at Kandy in Ceylon; and the opening by the Duke of Edinburgh of the new buildings of the University of Ceylon at Peradiniya.
ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT IN COMMONWEALTH COUNTRIES OVERSEAS

At the Commonwealth Economic Conference of December 1952 it was recognised that all the countries represented had a common interest in development policies in the Commonwealth. The Conference had before it the results of the examination into the prospects for development which was set in hand by the Conference of Commonwealth Finance Ministers which had met in London in January the same year.

The Commonwealth Economic Conference recorded in its communiqué the agreement of the Conference on the principle that "sound economic development should be encouraged with the object of increasing productive strength and competitive power, providing employment and raising the standards of life". The communiqué went on to lay down a general policy for development in the following terms:

"Development Policy. Throughout the Commonwealth there is wide scope for expanding the production of the essential supplies which the whole world needs—food and agricultural products, minerals and engineering products—and improving the means of transporting them. This development of the basic essentials has on occasion been impeded by other development of a less sound and permanent kind, which has overtaxed the countries' resources and has failed to contribute to the building of economic strength. The Conference agreed that in sterling area countries development should be concentrated on projects which directly or indirectly contribute to the improvement of the area's balance of payments with the rest of the world. Such projects should strengthen the economy of the countries concerned and increase their competitive power in world markets and so, by improving their balance of payments, bring increasing prosperity to their peoples. In some countries of the area, however, development plans have been or are being made to provide for some basic improvement in the standards of living, which is a necessary foundation for further economic development. Some social investment is also urgently needed in the more developed countries certain of which have rapidly increasing populations. The Conference recognised the need in such cases for these types of investment."

At the Conference of Commonwealth Finance Ministers held at Sydney in January 1954 the policy agreed at the Commonwealth Economic Conference was confirmed in the following terms:

"We have re-affirmed our determination to press on with development in conformity with our general policy to the limit of our available resources. In some of our countries private enterprise is making a large contribution to development. Governments will be concerned to promote balanced development whether public or private, to pursue policies designed to increase their own savings and to encourage private investment from both internal and external sources."

Most of the finance for the development of any country must normally come from its own internal resources; the development of most of the countries covered in this review has in fact so been financed in recent years. The United Kingdom has been traditionally the main source of external finance for investment in the other countries of the Commonwealth, although Canada has been and is now benefiting from a large inflow of United States' capital. At the Commonwealth Economic Conference in 1952 the United Kingdom Government gave certain undertakings which were set out in the following terms in the communiqué:

"The United Kingdom Government have undertaken to make a special effort to provide additional capital for Commonwealth development by facilitating the financing of schemes in other Commonwealth countries, which will con-
tribute to the improvement of the sterling area's balance of payments. The Conference took note that the United Kingdom Government would wish before making any of this additional finance available for Commonwealth development, to be sure that the country concerned was itself devoting an adequate part of its resources to investment designed to improve the sterling area's balance of payments and was ready to make a sufficient contribution towards the particular scheme in question, to ensure that both countries had an interest in seeing that it was carried through as efficiently and economically as possible."

In accordance with the terms of this undertaking, the United Kingdom Government have applied the following tests, sometimes known as "the criteria", in deciding whether to make capital available for investment in the Commonwealth:

(a) that the scheme in question will contribute to the improvement of the sterling area's balance of payments with the rest of the world;
(b) that the borrowing country is devoting an adequate part of its resources to such development;
(c) that the borrowing country will make an adequate contribution towards the particular scheme in question.

The adoption of these criteria in the provision of United Kingdom capital reflected the sterling Commonwealth's decision to concentrate on the kind of development which would strengthen the balance of payments of the sterling area. The criteria also provided a means of dividing fairly the available capital which was necessarily limited and likely to be less than the other Commonwealth countries would desire.

The special effort which the United Kingdom undertook to make to provide additional capital for Commonwealth development has taken a number of forms.

London Market borrowing by Governments. The London Market is the traditional source of capital investment for the Commonwealth and since the Commonwealth Economic Conference of 1952 the United Kingdom Government, in considering applications, have applied the principles mentioned above.

In practice, Commonwealth Governments seek both general development programme loans and particular assistance for individual projects; for example, the loan to Ceylon in 1954 was designed to secure current assistance under a longer-term programme for basic development; while the New Zealand Murupara loan was raised in 1953 to help with the finance for a specific project, the cost of which will be spread over a period of time. Total loans raised since 1951 on the London Market by Commonwealth Governments are as follows:

| (£ million) | 1951 | 1952 | 1953 | 1954 (Up to October) |
|-------------|------|------|------|---------------------|
| Ceylon      | ..   | ..   | ..   | 5                   |
| New Zealand | ..   | ..   | ..   | 10                  |
| Federation of Rhodesia and Nyasaland—Southern Rhodesia | .. | 5 | 7.5 | 10 | 10 |
| Northern Rhodesia | .. | 7.5 | — | 10 | 10 |

London Market borrowing—private. The general principles applicable to Government borrowing are also applicable to private money raised on the London Market. Applications to raise more than £50,000 on the London Market require the consent of the Treasury which is advised by the Capital Issues Committee. The applications from sterling Commonwealth countries approved in the course of 1953 totalled £40 million; similar records were not maintained for 1952 or earlier years and it is therefore not possible to provide a comparable figure before that date.

Other private borrowing. There is no exchange control in the United Kingdom on transfers of capital to the rest of the sterling area and there are no reliable estimates of the total amounts of investment moving from the United Kingdom to
other sterling Commonwealth countries. The Capital Issues control covers only part of such investment and there is other investment going on all the time. This includes both the investment of funds available from the existing resources of United Kingdom firms and also the ploughing back of profits in sterling Commonwealth countries.

The case of United Kingdom private investment in Canada is rather different because this investment represents dollar expenditure by the United Kingdom and is therefore a direct burden on the balance of payments. It has, however, been recognised that it is in the best interests of the United Kingdom to facilitate United Kingdom participation in Canadian development and the present policy as stated by the Economic Secretary to the Treasury is "to provide dollars within the limits of our resources for direct investment in productive enterprise which will benefit our balance of payments". During 1953 applications for new investment in Canada were approved to a total of approximately £38 million.

Commonwealth Development Finance Company. An additional channel for the provision of United Kingdom capital for development in the Commonwealth is the Commonwealth Development Finance Company, which was established at the time of the Commonwealth Economic Conference. (For details of this Company see page 40). Two examples of the kind of project in which the Company is interested are the Sui Gas Transmission Company's project in Pakistan for the piping of natural gas (to which the Commonwealth Development Finance Company has lent £1 million) and the South African Industrial Cellulose Corporation's project for the production of woodpulp from eucalyptus trees (to which the Commonwealth Development Finance Company is lending £1½ million).

Sterling lending by the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development. At the Commonwealth Economic Conference in December 1952, the United Kingdom Government indicated that as part of its special effort to provide additional capital for sound Commonwealth development it was contemplating the possibility of making available sterling for lending by the International Bank for this purpose. Subsequently the Chancellor of the Exchequer announced on 3rd February, 1953 that sterling for such loans would come from the United Kingdom subscription to the capital stock of the Bank and it was expected that some £60 million over six years would be available. So far it has been agreed to release £5 million sterling as part of a larger International Bank loan to the Indian Iron and Steel Company, and £5 million sterling as the International Bank loan to the Sui Gas Transmission Company in Pakistan.

£10 million Credit to Pakistan. In July 1953 agreement was reached on the grant of a credit of £10 million by the Export Credits Guarantee Department to Pakistan for the purchase of equipment in the United Kingdom for schemes directly or indirectly connected with food production.

Monies available under the Colonial Development and Welfare Acts. The United Kingdom is providing £140 million for planned development and welfare in its dependencies over a 10-year period ending 31st March, 1956; and arrangements for future years are now under consideration. In 1953 some £18.5 million was approved, mainly in the form of grants from this source for a variety of purposes in which communications figured prominently. The actual amount expended in 1953 was £14 million and the corresponding figure for 1952 was £15 million.

Colonial Development Corporation. By the Overseas Resources Development Act of 1948 the Colonial Development Corporation was given power to borrow up to £100 million for development schemes of a commercial nature in the United Kingdom dependencies. By the end of 1953 the Corporation had received capital sanction for expenditure of £48.5 million; in the year 1953 alone the amount sanctioned was £5.5 million, and the corresponding figure for 1952 was £7 million.

Another source of financial aid open to India, Pakistan and Ceylon was established in 1950 when the Colombo Plan was initiated. This Plan represented a special effort initiated by the Commonwealth to assist the economic development of South and South-East Asia as part of a co-operative enterprise, which now
includes not only Commonwealth members but also the foreign countries in the area. Further information about the Colombo Plan is given on pages 79, 80 and 84. As their capital and technical assistance under the Colombo Plan, the United Kingdom, Canada, Australia and New Zealand have made the following sums available:

**United Kingdom.** Arrangements have been made with India, Pakistan and Ceylon providing for releases of £210 million, £24 million and £21 million respectively from their blocked sterling balances. The releases are being spread over the six years to 30th June, 1957 (although the agreement with Ceylon provided for a release in 1950/51 as well) which means that the countries in question will have had access to about £42 million a year in that period. While these arrangements represent the repaying of debts arising during the war, they nevertheless impose an appreciable strain on the production resources of the United Kingdom. The United Kingdom has also promised to spend up to £2.8 million on technical assistance for South and South-East Asia in the six years to 30th June, 1957.

**Canada.** By June 1954 Canada had made available in a series of annual parliamentary votes a total of approximately $102 million. Of this amount about $100 million was for economic aid and $2.4 million for technical assistance. In addition Canada had made a special $5 million grant of wheat to Pakistan from which counterpart funds were generated for use on agreed development projects.

**Australia.** The Australian Government pledged a contribution of £A31.25 million over the period of the Colombo Plan to the capital aid programme of which £A17.8 million had either been spent or firmly committed to specific projects by June 1954. In addition up to £A3.5 million has been promised for technical assistance.

**New Zealand.** The New Zealand Government agreed to provide £3 million in capital assistance from 1951 to 1954. Of this £2,393,640 had been spent or earmarked for specific projects by June 1954. The remaining some £606,000 is now to be made available for allocation in 1954-55 together with supplementary funds to bring the total up to £1 million for that year. The New Zealand Government are also contributing £400,000 for technical assistance.

Under the Technical Co-operation Scheme of the Colombo Plan India, Pakistan and Ceylon have agreed to contribute £750,000, £161,290, and £400,000 respectively for technical assistance.

In addition to the capital provided from within the Commonwealth for its own development, capital has been provided by the International Bank, by various institutions and authorities in the United States and by a consortium of Swiss Banks.

**Lending other than Sterling by the International Bank.** By far the greater part of International Bank lending to the Commonwealth has been in dollars, but some loans include other currencies (apart from sterling). This lending has been playing a significant part in helping to finance fairly large scale industrial projects, or sectors of development programmes such as electricity or transport, in Ceylon, India, South Africa, Northern and Southern Rhodesia and Pakistan. Loans to Australia have also been made, but have been less specifically applicable to individual projects or sectors of programmes, and have been used to finance the import of equipment and materials required for various parts of Australian State and Commonwealth development programmes. Details of such loans are as follows:
